The Leaders AI Innovation Needs
2026-09-08

The Leaders AI Innovation Needs

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings For over 25 years, I have studied exceptional leaders of innovation, individuals from a range of companies and countries, from the Cleveland Clinic Abu Dhabi to Pixar. For hundreds of hours, I have observed leaders who have figured out how to get their colleagues to adopt emerging technologies to innovate and change the game for their customers. What I have learned from doing this work is this: leading AI innovation is not about having a vision and getting others to follow you to the future; rather, it is about inviting others to co-create the future with you. This is also true of the AI age. While virtually every company is scrambling to adopt new AI practices, the leaders who have made the most progress are those who believe the value of AI will come from using the technology to do work people could never do themselves, and the least value will come from simply swapping machines for people. Their goal is to pursue solutions that augment human ingenuity. And they appreciate that their toughest challenge is about people, not technology. These leaders know that we all need help navigating uncertainty, and they encourage others to try out new ways of working despite real intellectual and emotional demands. Innovation is rarely the feat of a solo genius but rather the fruit of co-creation, which requires collaboration, experimentation, and learning from people with differing expertise and perspectives. Collaboration entails weaving together diversity of thought through constructive conflict to create a marketplace of ideas. Experimentation involves actively testing, pivoting, and refining. And learning happens when individuals and collectives reflect on what they have gleaned from those experiments—and then adapt their course of action. To generate and scale innovative AI solutions, leaders rely on a repertoire of interrelated roles, what we refer to as the “ABCs” of leadership: architects, bridgers, and catalysts. Architects know they cannot mandate innovation; they foster the culture and capabilities that enable co-creation. These leaders start by raising their organization’s collective aspirations through a shared sense of purpose and values. By re-shaping the social environment of their organizations, these leaders encourage their colleagues to work through the inevitable conflicts of collaborating with others and the fear of failure. They reward thoughtful risk-taking, treat intelligent failures as learning opportunities, and provide people the tools, data, and perhaps most importantly, the permission to try. Bridgers work at the boundaries of their enterprises, building trust-based partnerships with those outside their walls. No company, no matter how well-resourced, has all the talent and tools they need for innovation given the unprecedented pace at which technology is advancing. With AI, I hear that there is a shortage of individuals who know how to translate and work across technology and business. Technical experts who are developing AI solutions typically do not have the contextual intelligence about the realities of business and what customers want, while the businesspeople do not appreciate the opportunities and risks of implementing the technology. Catalysts have to mobilize stakeholders across whole industries, sectors, or nations to address the most audacious innovation challenges. Catalysts are also essential if we are to establish the global norms and guardrails that keep AI aligned with human interests. They can bring in overlooked voices, surface second-order effects, and help mitigate AI risks and share the benefits of AI more equitably. Many leaders must be prepared to reinvent themselves if they hope not only to generate AI innovative solutions but actually turn them into reality—launch or commercialize them so they have the desired impact. They must give up being the smartest person in the room and get comfortable collaborating, experimenting, and learning with “unusual suspects.” Even AI-first executives admit we do not know where AI will take us nor how we are going to get there. The winners of the AI race will be the companies whose leaders accept that their task is wayfinding. Leaders must enable their colleagues to have the courage and curiosity of explorers who once crossed the uncharted oceans without clarity about their destination. Fortunately, we have access to more sophisticated tools than the ancient seafarers. For AI to make a positive difference for customers and society, it will take individuals at all levels of our organizations to be willing and able to join the journey. Exclusive: Obscure Committee Set To Be at Center of Trump Fight if Democrats Win House

Exclusive: Obscure Committee Set To Be at Center of Trump Fight if Democrats Win House

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Chairman Bruce Westerman, R-Ark., speaks as ranking member Jared Huffman, D-Calif., listens during a House Natural Resources Committee markup on Tuesday, May 6, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If Democrats win control of the House in November, party leaders plan to turn the final two years of the Trump era into a barrage of investigations over alleged corruption and contempt for the law. Much of the scrutiny would be expected to come from the Judiciary and Oversight Committees, the traditional engines of congressional investigations. But another, less prominent committee could emerge as an unlikely center of the fight: the House Natural Resources Committee. The 45-member panel has oversight over virtually all federal lands, giving it jurisdiction over a striking collection of controversies that have defined President Trump’s second term. That includes his efforts to remake Washington, D.C. by building a White House ballroom and a triumphal arch, and the troubled renovation of the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool. It also covers the Administration’s efforts to open up national parks to an unprecedented amount of hunting, fishing and drilling, as well as a recent proposal to give a swath of California’s Yosemite National Park to a private developer. “We are no longer a backwater here at the Natural Resources Committee,” Rep. Jared Huffman, the committee’s top Democrat, tells TIME. “You check a lot of boxes with the Natural Resources Committee in terms of some of the most high-profile controversies in this Administration.” Huffman, who is likely to become the committee’s chair in a Democratic House, spoke to TIME this month from his Capitol Hill office decorated with paintings from the Muir Woods National Monument, the Golden Gate National Recreation Area, and other public lands in his coastal California district. Leading the House Natural Resources Committee has been the ultimate goal for the former environmental lawyer since he joined the House in 2013. “This is the top of the mountain for me,” he says. “This is what I always wanted.” Huffman has a running list of the investigations he would pursue if he takes the gavel. His plans would amount to a striking transformation for a committee more commonly associated with debates over endangered species, grazing rights, and federal water projects. Since Trump returned to office, committee Democrats have tried to investigate and call attention to Administration actions while lacking the authority to compel cooperation. With control of the House, those requests could be backed by subpoenas, sworn depositions, and public testimony from Administration officials, contractors, and private companies. Huffman says he expects a wave of government employees to approach the committee as whistleblowers, feeling more confident that Democrats will act on their concerns. “There are principled, patriotic Americans working in all of these federal agencies that are horrified with the corruption and illegality that’s been happening around them,” he says. “Some of them have already been helpful, but I think many, many more will come forward under a Democratic majority.” Read more: The Democrat Ready to Beef Up Pentagon Oversight if the House Flips The Trump Administration says the changes to federally controlled land are intended to make the District of Columbia “safe and beautiful” by restoring monuments, cleaning up federal parkland, and improving public safety. Under its current rules, the Natural Resources chairman has broad authority to issue subpoenas for testimony and documents. The precise scope of that position’s power in the next Congress would depend on the rules adopted by the House and the committee next year. The Trump Administration could also refuse to comply with some subpoenas, producing lengthy court battles. Huffman is perhaps best known on Capitol Hill as the founder of the Freethought Caucus, which counts 37 members and advocates for church-state separation and true religious freedom. Last month, he published a book, No Prophets: The Fight to Save Democracy from Christian Nationalism. But if he gets the gavel, his work investigating the Administration would shift to the forefront. Huffman says he intends for the committee to revisit many of the Administration’s decisions from the previous two years. “We will not only be doing the oversight that presents itself on our watch,” he says. “We will be doing two years of catch-up for a Republican Congress that looked the other way in the face of all this corruption, self-enrichment and illegality.” The committee’s most visible investigations could target what Huffman calls Trump’s “vanity projects” including the $400 million, 90,000 sq. ft. White House ballroom being built on the site of the demolished East Wing. Last month, the Supreme Court allowed the construction to move forward, after a lower court had halted the work saying Trump did not have the authority to build the ballroom without congressional approval. The President is also set to break ground on a 250-foot “triumphal arch” near the Arlington National Cemetery that would be taller than the Arc de Triomphe in Paris. He has also proposed erecting hundreds of statues in a Garden of American Heroes and transforming the East Potomac Golf Links into a championship-level course. Some of the projects have moved forward without congressional authorization or have been reviewed by commissions that Trump has filled with allies. The Administration has maintained that the work is legal and will restore grandeur to the capital. "It's a dramatic abuse of the public trust for one man's ego,” Huffman says of the projects. “It's also a direct attack on the rule of law because we have long had laws like the Commemorative Works Act that spell out in great detail how monuments and other structures in the nation's capital get authorized and built.” Huffman says that if Democrats win the House the committee would seek records showing how the projects were approved, how much public money had been spent, and whether donors received or sought favorable treatment from the Administration. The private financing, he says, would receive particular scrutiny. He wants to know who donated, how much they contributed and what business they had before the federal government. Last year, the White House released a partial list of ballroom donors, including crypto and tech billionaires and defense contractors, but has not revealed how much each donor is giving. Huffman also questioned why money and Park Service personnel were being concentrated in Washington while the Administration was cutting staff and services elsewhere in the national park system. “You could look at it and say, well, you know, why die on that hill? These are just little pet projects of Donald Trump,” Huffman says. “But this is real money that is being misprioritized. It’s an abuse of the public trust.” Another Washington project he wants to investigate is the renovation of the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool. Trump ordered the pool’s bottom covered with a blue coating as part of a rehabilitation that cost more than $14 million. Soon after the project was completed, algae returned and sections of the new coating began to peel. The President blamed vandalism. In July, the Administration dropped a felony charge against a U.S. Olympic canoeist it had blamed for the damage, and acknowledged in court that the damage was “a result of flawed installation by the contractor." Huffman says he expects whistleblowers inside the Park Service to provide additional information about how the contracts for the pool project were awarded and supervised, when officials learned that the work was failing, and whether employees were pressured to misrepresent what had occurred. “The fact that National Park Service employees have been told to lie about what happened, for example, we obviously want a full accounting,” he says. (The White House did not respond to Huffman’s allegation that Park Service employees were instructed to lie). If federal agencies refuse to provide the committee with records, Huffman adds, he would be prepared to issue subpoenas. “You walk softly and carry a big stick,” he says. “The subpoena is your big stick. You hope not to use it, but I think it’s very good to have.” The recent bipartisan outcry over the prospects of a private developer gaining a swath of Yosemite National Park illustrates how the committee’s work could shift dramatically under Democratic rule. The current chairman, Republican Rep. Bruce Westerman of Arkansas, has not announced any committee action regarding the Administration’s proposal to a land swap intended to help Kingsbarn Realty Capital, a private real estate firm, build a road between property it owns outside Yosemite and roads inside the national park. The company plans an upscale development of cabins, homes, and commercial buildings on two parcels near the park’s western boundary. “I don’t like what it would do on its own terms to Yosemite,” Huffman says. “But I especially don’t like the precedent, because if they’re allowed to do it, this will be coming to a national park near you in some other form.” Under the proposal, the government would transfer a small parcel of park property in exchange for land of comparable value that Kingsbarn would acquire elsewhere in California. The company has argued that the shorter route would reduce driving and pollution. The Interior Department has said no decision has been made and that any exchange would have to comply with federal law, environmental reviews and public-notification requirements. It has denied reports that political appointees improperly pressured the Park Service to advance the proposal. Huffman says if he becomes Natural Resources chair, he intends to seek communications between the developer, the White House, Interior officials and Park Service employees. Investigators would look for evidence that political or financial ties influenced the process, including any connections between the company and Trump or his family. Campaign finance records show Kingsbarn’s chief executive, Jeff Pori, making more than $4,000 in contributions to Trump's campaign in 2024. The company’s representative, Lanny Davis, has said politics had nothing to do with the proposal. But Huffman remains skeptical.  “You’ve got to dig into the money trail,” Huffman says. “You’ve got to figure out who’s been going to Mar-a-Lago and paying tribute to the Trump family.” Huffman says he would also probe the Administration’s “war on clean energy,” including its terminating offshore wind leases and redirecting money toward fossil-fuel investments. Interior officials have defended the agreements as a way to dispose of projects they regard as unreliable and economically unworkable. Democrats have questioned whether the Administration had the authority to make the deals and whether taxpayer money or federal lease payments were improperly used. Other investigations would involve government investments, loans and equity stakes in mineral companies. Huffman says he would examine how companies were selected, whether federal safeguards were followed and whether Trump’s family or associates stood to gain financially. He adds that investigations could not consume the committee’s entire agenda. He plans to pursue legislation on environmental permitting, public lands and energy, as well as tribal water settlements, health care, education and public safety. Citing the committee’s preview over Native American land and communities, he says it may also examine prediction markets such as Kalshi, which tribal leaders contend are encroaching on gaming revenue used to finance government services. “It's going to be an enormously busy Congress,” Huffman says if Democrats win the House, as most polls suggest. “The whiplash when we go from this to what will be a very fast-moving and unrelenting workload demand—I mean, it's going to be a shock for a lot of members. It's going to challenge me. It's going to challenge all of us.”

Iran Threatens to Target U.S. Energy Assets in Gulf If U.S. Strikes Again

TIME | 2026-09-08

Update your preferences in Account Settings Men gather at the damage to a storage and warehousing facility at the port of Kuhestak following a U.S. strike in Iran's southern Hormozgan province on Sept. 6, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Iran warned on Monday that it could target U.S. oil and gas interests in the Gulf if the U.S. launches any new attacks on its assets. Responding to U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s warning that “if Iran shoots at U.S. ships, we will destroy (and sink) their oil tankers,” Iranian Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf said: “It’s simple: the oil and gas production chain here is sprawling, accessible, and exposed. American oil and gas companies across these waters and facilities share that exposure. Strike our assets and you get struck." Ghalibaf’s warning comes as Tehran seeks greater leverage amid Washington’s ongoing economic pressure campaign and stalled efforts to negotiate an end to the war that the U.S. and Israel launched on Feb. 28. Mohsen Rezaei, Iran’s Supreme National Security Council Secretary, said Monday that "economic warfare will be met by a maritime exclusion zone across the Persian Gulf to the blockade perimeter." The "exclusion zone” in the Persian Gulf and Gulf of Oman would begin at the blockade line, extend through the Strait of Hormuz, and continue into the Persian Gulf, Rezaei said. Vessels entering the zone to transit the Strait would be placed on an Iranian sanctions list. The latest flare-up began Saturday, when Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) attacked U.S. warships in the region. The U.S. responded by striking three Iranian oil tankers, including one near Kharg Island, Iran’s main oil export hub. The U.S. and Iran have for the past week exchanged a flurry of attacks, ending a brief respite in active hostilities. Oil prices reached near six-week highs on Monday as commercial traffic through the Strait of Hormuz, a narrow waterway through which a fifth of global oil and liquefied natural gas shipments flowed before the war, has fallen sharply. Diplomatic efforts between the U.S. and Iran have stalled. The two countries signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) on June 17, agreeing to reopen the Strait of Hormuz and engage in technical talks to end the war. But the truce was short-lived. After a commercial vessel was struck in the Strait in late June, the U.S. attacked Iranian missile storage and radar sites, prompting Iran to retaliate against U.S. military installations in the region. Intermittent attacks continued in July, and fighting resumed in late August after a brief, informal pause. The Trump Administration has said it is investigating a U.S. strike last week on a family compound in southern Iran where a wedding celebration was underway, killing at least five people and wounding more than 60, according to Iranian state media. No new formal negotiations have been announced since the MOU’s 60-day deadline expired on Aug. 17. Although some of Iran’s top officials, including President Masoud Pezeshkian, have signaled a willingness to return to the MOU and resume talks with the U.S., the two countries’ positions remain far apart. The U.S. wants to significantly restrict or dismantle Iran’s nuclear program and guarantee unrestricted commercial passage through the Strait of Hormuz. Iran has called for broad sanctions relief, access to frozen assets, an end to the U.S. naval blockade of Iranian ports, and a role in managing the Strait. Hard-liners in Iran have shown little appetite for compromise on those red lines, and analysts previously told TIME that Iranian leaders may believe time is on their side, with Tehran prepared to absorb further economic and military pressure in hopes that rising energy prices will eventually prove too politically costly to U.S. President Donald Trump. “The Iranian nation accepts neither threats nor coercion, nor negotiations whose outcome would be the weakening of the country’s rights,” Hossein Taeb, head of the IRGC’s Basij paramilitary force, said on Monday. “Negotiations are for securing the nation’s rights, not surrendering them.” While Trump faces domestic pressure to rein in gas prices and avoid an open-ended war in the Middle East ahead of the November midterms, he has also appeared determined to secure an outcome he can present as a victory. “I’m not trying to force Iran to the bargaining table,” Trump posted on Truth Social last week. “I like our position now much better, with almost total control of the Hormuz Strait, and their economy totally collapsing. They are just playing out the inevitable.” Iran is planning to establish a new restricted zone to amp up global economic pressure and force an end to the U.S. blockade. At the same time, Iran and Oman have agreed on a temporary shipping corridor through the Strait of Hormuz and are in talks on its future administration—an arrangement that could formalize Tehran’s role in managing the waterway against U.S. opposition. But Ghalibaf acknowledged in parliament on Sunday that Iran is struggling with sharp inflation, a collapsing rial, and high unemployment. Washington’s economic pressure campaign, which includes the naval blockade, sanctions on Iran, and the threat of secondary sanctions on Iran’s trading partners, has squeezed Tehran. The International Monetary Fund estimates that inflation in Iran is near 70%. The Strait of Hormuz, which Iran began restricting at the start of the war, has been the country’s most important bargaining chip. But that leverage may be weakening as Gulf states turn to routes that bypass the Strait and the U.S. escorts shipments through the corridor. The conflict has also inflicted mounting costs on Iran’s own economy by trapping tens of millions of barrels of Iranian crude inside the Gulf. Brent Crude oil prices approached the $100 per barrel benchmark on Tuesday, reaching near six-week highs of $98.51, as traffic via the Strait of Hormuz remains severely disrupted. Kpler, a commodities data and analytics firm, shared data with TIME that shows only 12 ships crossed the Strait on Monday, significantly lower than the 138 vessels that typically passed through the vital trade waterway during a 24-hour period before the war. Meanwhile, President Donald Trump on Monday said that “oil prices will drop precipitously" when the U.S. "win[s] the war," but he did not provide a timeline or address the likelihood of energy markets remaining volatile even after the conflict is said to have ended. The U.K. is reimposing major economic sanctions against Iran in line with the European Union and the United States, it was announced Tuesday. The legislation expands trade prohibitions against Iran to additional goods, technology, and services. Financial measures will "further reduce the government of Iran’s ability to access the U.K. financial system and raise funds in support of its nuclear programme," according to Minister for the Middle East Stephen Doughty. Additionally, British Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband announced the U.K. will take action this week to "report Iran to the U.N. Security Council for its nuclear violations, alongside our allies."

The AfD Is Rising. Germany’s Political Center Is Failing.
2026-09-07

The AfD Is Rising. Germany’s Political Center Is Failing.

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Leaders of the far-right Alternative for Germany party celebrating first exit poll win to AFD following state elections in Saxony-Anhalt on September 6, 2026 in Magdeburg, Germany. An electoral earthquake has fractured Germany’s societal and political tectonic plates. In a state election on Sunday, the far-right Alternative for Germany party won 43.8% of the vote in Saxony-Anhalt, a state of roughly two million people in the eastern part of the country. When Friedrich Merz first campaigned to lead Germany’s conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU), in 2018, he vowed to reduce support for the far-right AfD in half. In a bitter irony, Chancellor Merz’s CDU lost half its support in Saxony-Anhalt and after its resounding victory the AfD has become a direct threat to his leadership, and is beginning to overturn Germany’s postwar political order. The drubbing in Saxony-Anhalt may be only the beginning. Two more regional elections are scheduled later this month and could demonstrate the growing strength of Germany’s fringe parties and intensify pressure on Merz’s governing coalition in Berlin. The AfD is more than a political disruptor. German domestic intelligence has classified parts of the party, including its Saxony-Anhalt chapter, as right-wing extremist. Yet the AfD has overtaken the CDU in national polls, while Merz’s approval ratings have fallen to levels unprecedented for a German chancellor. Although Merz has backtracked on campaign promises, most notably his commitment to uphold the debt brake—loosening the constitutional limit on government borrowing—he has remained resolute in preserving the “firewall” between the CDU and AfD. That firewall is intended to keep Germany anchored in its postwar democracy deliberately constructed with guardrails against its darkest history to prevent backsliding into authoritarianism. The AfD has used Merz’s flip flop on the debt brake against him and is questioning defense spending on Ukraine. Upon taking office in May 2025, a little over a year ago, Merz promised to revive Germany’s stagnant economy and increase defense spending to position the country as Europe’s de facto military leader. An antagonistic Russia and an unreliable America have left Germany with few alternatives. German businesses are suffering with high costs, American tariffs and intensifying competition from China. Merz also made another defining promise: to beat back the AfD and restore the centrist CDU’s  claim to being the true conservative party in Germany. Instead, the election in Saxony-Anhalt will amplify calls, including from within his own party, for him to step aside. Merz’s blunders over the past year cannot be dismissed as the mistakes of a political novice. He is a seasoned operator who, unlike many of his German peers, has extensive experience in the private sector. He promised an ambitious program of reforms to tackle Germany’s welfare and pension programs, but his proposals were too tepid and too late. And his poor communication style has also overshadowed his efforts on curbing migration and his diplomatic campaign across Europe to sustain support for Ukraine. The CDU’s predicament is not surprising. Merz has never been a particularly beloved figure, and the grand coalition between the conservatives and the Social Democratic Party was hardly the outcome German voters desired after the federal election last year. After the Federal Republic of Germany was established in 1949, there had been only  single Grand Coalition government during the first four decades. But Merz’s government is the fourth Grand Coalition government in Germany in the last two decades. This coalition, was supposed to be different: it was formed to enact far-reaching reforms and hold the AfD at bay. So far, it has done neither. The AfD began in 2013 as a movement of anti-euro skeptics. It has since become a nationwide force, building its constituency through relentless opposition to immigration, nativist appeal and efforts to relativize Germany’s Nazi past. An anemic German economy and frustration with the established parties have accelerated its rise. The AfD is now the leading political party in Germany with support from nearly one-third of German voters. Like its far-right counterparts elsewhere in Europe, the AfD has prospered by channeling voters’ anger and grievance. Until now, however, it has remained in opposition. No other German party has been willing to forge a parliamentary coalition with the AfD, and even some European far-right parties consider the AfD so radical that they prefer to keep it at arm’s length. The AfD’s victory in Saxony-Anhalt and other upcoming regional elections could change that dynamic. Although the CDU and the AfD hold overlapping positions on migration and some social questions, the AfD’s hostility toward Germany’s culture of historical remembrance is fundamentally incompatible with the country’s center-right tradition. The CDU’s first leader, Konrad Adenauer, committed Germany to integration with the West—a principle known as Westbindung—that the AfD would dismantle. Merz has steadfastly defended that orientation. The AfD, by contrast, seeks renewed ties with Russia and Germany’s withdrawal from the euro, which it presents as a path to economic revival. The mood of the country can now be read most clearly in the regional elections, where voters are preoccupied with jobs, immigration, schools and public services. These are precisely the areas in which the grand coalition that Merz leads is coming up short. The AfD offers a story of national decline but few credible solutions and no meaningful experience of governing. Yet criticizing the AfD is not sufficient for the CDU. The democratic answer to the rise of the AfD must be responsible government, solving complex problems through the institutions and the practices of a healthy democracy. Germany’s response matters beyond its borders. Across the Atlantic world, Germany's confrontation with extreme nationalist movements is being observed through the light of its history. Germany stands as a reminder of how swiftly democratic norms can erode and an example of a nation that has spent eight decades trying to preserve the lessons of that collapse. Merz may not be able to revive his personal fortunes, but he can still shape his  legacy by reminding Germans of their particular duty to defend democracy. The most effective answer to the AfD is not moral condemnation alone but competent government and courageous leadership. Boldness may not win Merz a second term, but it could help arrest the rise of the AfD.
The Atlantic
The Novel That Looks Death in the Eye
2026-09-08

The Novel That Looks Death in the Eye

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The human brain has a method for dealing with the intrinsic fear of death. Terror-management theory, which builds on the work of the cultural anthropologist Ernest Becker, explains that the psyche handles this fear in a variety of ways. Many people either escape reality or adopt a belief system that promises immortality: so on the one hand, sex, drugs, or entertainment; on the other, religion, fame, or careerism. Or we might immerse ourselves in family, or culture—something that imbues life with meaning. Otherwise, the theory goes, we would be cognitively immobilized by impending doom. In her gut-twisting third novel, Switzy, Emma Cline does not abide by the tenets of terror management. Instead, she pulls death out from behind the curtain and forces her readers to look it in the eye for more than 300 pages. Switzy follows the final few days in the life of David Hastings, a former high-flying executive who’s been forced into retirement, and obsolescence, by an Alzheimer’s diagnosis. He has taken advantage of Switzerland’s right-to-die laws, paying the fees (a paltry sum for a wealthy man) and filling out the required paperwork. Now he’ll swallow some barbiturates and die in an impersonal room just outside Zurich, far from his family and his home in America. Cline’s approach is merciless—not toward David, for whom she shows tenderness and grace, but toward the reader, who will be nearly flattened by this novel. Or so I sincerely hope, because death ought to flatten us once in a while. Switzy begins mid-flight, as David hurtles across the Atlantic in a friend’s private jet. Accompanied by Cody—a former corporate underling who now functions as David’s caretaker, body man, and secretary—he lands first in London. There, he has dinner with his only child, an emotionally distant daughter who doesn’t know about her father’s decline, let alone his upcoming euthanasia. David tours a museum, visits his grandson, reboards the plane for his final flight, to Zurich; he dazedly lets Cody steer him around as a parent would guide a toddler. The younger man negotiates the intricacies of the world on David’s behalf, buffering him from the demands of day-to-day life—buying a museum ticket, sending a text message, procuring beverages. David needs the help: He is at times absent in his own mind. As rendered by Cline in a wandering, dreamlike style, this becomes a startling and eerie phenomenon—like the moment you realize you’ve been driving for miles but don’t recall anything about the journey. Far from experiencing the blissful ignorance that some people might project onto dementia patients, David is all too aware that his brain has locked away basic memories and social customs. He writes his grandson’s name, Rainer, in a notebook so he can recall it; a clock “in big kindergarten letters” informs him of the date and time each morning; he stares too long at someone in the museum and provokes his annoyance. And he sees each gap, knows that it signals another loss. “Work,” he thinks as he chews a croissant, “everything seemed like work. Even these good things—even this buttered mouthful.” Read: A different way to think about medicine’s most stubborn enigma The first half of Switzy crawls by, but it feels achingly necessary. This is time the way David experiences it, slowed down and sometimes skipped over: Alzheimer’s novels are rare, and those told from the patient’s point of view are even rarer. Cline, always a graceful stylist, employs a kind of abbreviated modernism to leap among David’s thoughts. They skitter backwards to memories of his failed marriage and his time at a boys’ boarding school. They run in circles, around catchy sayings—such as a mnemonic about Henry VIII’s abbreviated marriages, “Divorced, beheaded, died; divorced, beheaded, survived”—that Alzheimer’s hasn’t stolen from him. And they jump ahead to the moment and manner of his death, which inches closer with every turned page. Cline’s last novel, The Guest, also followed a character who hastily approaches a reckoning. Alex, a poor and wily 22-year-old sex worker who has just been kicked out of the home of her most recent catch, Simon, has to make it through five days in the Hamptons, until she can plead to be let back into the high-end milieu of Simon’s Labor Day party. Dead-eyed and mutable, Alex, like David, is trying to put on just enough of a show to pass muster with the people around her. For her, a different kind of death awaits—complete impoverishment, and removal from a perch where she may or may not belong. Switzy raises the stakes on this kind of quest. As the novel’s second half picks up steam, so does the cadence of David’s confrontations with the fact of his looming extinction. When the plane lands in Zurich, an official comes on to inspect their passports. “David smiled to himself. It was obvious, wasn’t it? This man was Death. Why else would he know to come right onboard?” Soon after, he sees Death again as a waiter, a busboy, two restaurant patrons. On his last night, he wakes repeatedly in his hotel, checking the sky’s brightness as a sign of how much time he has left. An Omega watch advertisement catches his eye, and though he doesn’t say it, the meaning is clear: Omega is the last letter in the Greek alphabet, the universal symbol of finality. As long as David cannot stop returning to his ticking clock, neither can you. This kind of sustained attention to the one topic many people spend their lives avoiding is practically subversive—a thrill, really. And it’s possibly a public service too. Studies have repeatedly shown that people who think about death on a regular basis are happier, because contemplating mortality reinforces the idea, as Emily Dickinson put it, “That it will never come again/ Is what makes life so sweet.” I daresay most readers won’t feel much happiness reading Switzy—though it does have glimmers of humor—but they might experience moments of sublime clarity. Death functions here in a way that isn’t common in fiction. Its presence doesn’t make this novel a tearjerker—it’s not, for example, Maggie O’Farrell’s Hamnet, which beautifully laments the loss of a child. It offers no spectacle akin to Anne Boleyn’s trembling march to the scaffold at the end of Hilary Mantel’s singular historical novel, Bring Up the Bodies. Unlike many 19th-century novels, Switzy doesn’t use death to solve a social problem, à la Anna Karenina (romantic devastation) and Madame Bovary (financial ruin). Nor is death here a handy plot device, as it is in so many works across the past century. Switzy is not about the living who are left behind. Cline’s approach to death marks different territory: It’s about raw, undeniable truth. Read: Charles Manson, the girls, and the banality of desire Dying is a transformative event: People pass from the only reality they’ve ever been able to recognize into something nebulous and ungraspable (even for the most fervent religious believers). And this might sound obvious, but you, reader, will die. So will I. Much of humanity has raised its defenses high against death, perhaps unintentionally, as Ernest Becker proposes. So how will it feel to see a man marching straight toward it—not unflinchingly, or tremulously, but with a perfectly ordinary mixture of confusion and acceptance? In Switzy’s final pages, David becomes a man who cannot help but be alive, even in his last moments. He doesn’t want to eat breakfast, but he feels it’s “better to have something in the stomach.” He feels irritated that he is given a blue pen to sign documents—“blue ink was for grocery lists.” He fumbles over his last words. Cline writes, “His was both the realest life in the room and the most unreal.” The line between everything and nothing is as thin as a breath of air. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.
The Atlantic
The Myth of Perfect Knowledge
2026-09-08

The Myth of Perfect Knowledge

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The modern world owes a lot to the Enlightenment, the 18th-century intellectual movement that emphasized the power of reason. But the Enlightenment also created a myth: that we can get to know the objective world completely, with total clarity, as if we were looking at it from the outside. This myth is challenged by the Enlightenment’s greatest legacy—modern science. Reality is more complex than we expected. Physics—my trade, and perhaps the most objective among the sciences—questions the possibility of a full description of how things objectively are. An electron can be in a “quantum superposition,” so that to an observer, it doesn’t occupy just one location—and so can you, my reader. This does not mean that you could perceive being dislocated between two positions; as perceived by yourself, you are always in just one place. But an object’s properties may differ depending on who, or what, is looking at them—or may not even be defined at all. Properties are not simple features of things: They are relations between one thing and another. What we call “the world” is therefore only how some parts of reality manifest themselves to other parts—us, for instance. Humanity has always been aware that our understanding is partial and biased. But the fact that an objective and knowable reality is incompatible with the grammar of physics is astonishing. The Enlightenment could consider the mind as being outside the world, in an abstract realm of reason where knowledge resides. But human minds are not outside reality, watching it from its exterior. We, like everything else, are part of the very nature we study. Our knowledge is only one example of the many correlations that intertwine different parts of nature. In the early 20th century, Niels Bohr, a founder of quantum theory, realized that quantum phenomena indicate that we cannot discard our own physical nature when we observe the world. The ideal of grasping objective reality by making the observer irrelevant is incompatible with physics. We are quantum processes interacting with other quantum processes. Our knowledge is a rich but incomplete set of correlations between one piece of the world–ourselves–and the rest of it. Adam Frank: The truth physics can no longer ignore To see this fact from another point of view, consider saying “It’s raining and Ann does not know it.” This makes sense: You know that it’s raining and that Ann doesn’t. But if you replace “Ann” with a first-person pronoun, the result is odd: “It’s raining and I do not know it.” How can you declare that it’s raining if you do not know it? This observation points to the fact that every proposition can only express the knowledge of the speaker or writer. No proposition is asserted from outside reality, because there is nothing outside reality. To say “It is raining” is really to say: “I, as part of reality, know that it’s raining.” Objective statements independent of any knower are fictions. Purely objective reality is a useful myth, but only a myth. Anything said about the state of the world comes from someone’s singular perspective. This means that all of our knowledge is intrinsically uncertain. All statements have unavoidable margins of uncertainty. Indeed, we can be wrong about anything, even the basic facts of our own lives. My father grew up sure that his first name was Franco until he asked for a copy of his birth certificate and found out that my grandfather, without telling my grandma, had registered him as Giulio. He had been wrong about his own name. Humanity has been collectively wrong over and over again. We thought the sun moved around the earth, then learned that it is the earth that orbits the sun. Great scientists are even more likely to be wrong than others, because they have the courage to try—Einstein’s published work includes many mistakes. But in science, as in daily life, we do not need certainty; being reasonably sure is good enough. If I have an airplane to catch, I will go to the airport even though I’m not totally sure that the flight will take off; if I waited for certainty, I’d never leave home. Modern science offers an enormous body of knowledge that is effective and, in many domains, far better than all the alternatives, but it gives us no absolute certainty, no ultimate story, no Truth with a capital T. Within science, there are different ways of describing the same things. A glass of water can be described in terms of physics (its mass, velocity), thermodynamics (its temperature), chemistry (there is more than just H2O in any glass of water), biology (bacteria swim in it), particle physics (its electrons, protons, and neutrons), psychology (“Why did I fill the glass?”), economics (its price), and more. Each account is valid in its own terms; each reflects a way in which the same object interacts with different parts of reality, from atoms to the global economy. But none exhausts an “ultimate truth” of the glass. The same is true for human beings. I can think of you, my reader, as a body, as a mind, or as a soul. These aren’t different entities: They are the same entity, described in different languages, using different conceptual schemes, focusing on different aspects of the same you and different ways in which you interact with other parts of reality. There is no sharp separation or metaphysical gap between body and soul; matter and spirit; the physical world and the world of laws, ideas, and norms. In this sense, all things are equal: They are all aspects of the same natural world, considered from different perspectives, none of which is the ultimate one. Everything can be considered fundamental from one perspective or another. The expression “the equality of all things” comes from the title of the second chapter of The Book of Zhuangzi, one of the greatest books of humankind—an ancient classic of Chinese philosophy that articulates a similar intriguing perspectival and anti-foundational naturalism. This is the view to which I think modern science leads us. Many people resist it, some out of fear of losing solid reference points: objectivity, certainties, absolute morality, an immortal soul. But scientific knowledge does not become less valuable if you remain open to doubt. Morality does not lose power if you recognize that it is rooted in our biology, culture, and history. Our values do not weaken because they are rooted in what we are, not in something outside us. And life is even more beautiful and precious without dreams of immortality. We are not—as French existentialism described us—thrown into alien nature. In nature, we are home. In fact, it seems to me that recognizing the perspectival aspect of knowledge, values, and beliefs, and of reality itself, could help us become a bit less arrogant, a bit humbler and wiser. Conflicts between individuals or countries don’t stem only from greed and thirst for power. They are also fueled by the belief that we are objectively right and the enemy is objectively wrong, which ignores the plurality of perspectives. We have our values that guide us, but understanding the perspective of others is the first step toward wisdom—and toward peace. In our era of nuclear armaments, ignoring that others have different perspectives, and failing to respect them, could drag the world into a catastrophe.
The Atlantic
Could High-Tech Toys Help Animals Act More Wild?
2026-09-08

Could High-Tech Toys Help Animals Act More Wild?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Hudson, a 10-year-old arctic fox at Massachusetts’s Stone Zoo, was not doing all that much when his caretakers came by his enclosure one sunny morning last month. But the moment he detected their approach, he scurried over. His eyes focused on the pink, kibble-filled puzzle box they were holding—a programmable, lab-made device that’s part of an experiment in letting captive animals play around with technology. In the past year, in partnership with a team led by Rébecca Kleinberger, an engineer and a computer scientist at Northeastern University, Stone Zoo has introduced Hudson to his puzzle feeder, two black bears to a motion-sensitive bubble machine, a family of colobus monkeys to an interactive cabinet of mirrors, and more. The zoo is not alone in its tech curiosity. At the Maryland Zoo, caretakers have debuted a hunting simulation for polar bears, in which a robot arm extends and retracts a romaine leaf smeared with peanut butter—mimicking a prey animal poking its head out of an ice hole. At Zoo Atlanta, keepers have partnered with engineers at the Georgia Institute of Technology to develop an interactive audio wall where elephants can insert their trunks into motion-sensitive holes to trigger a variety of low-frequency sounds. At some facilities, penguins have been given access to iPad games designed for pet cats, and orangutans have been taught to play on an Xbox. Hudson the arctic fox makes a case for the benefits that captive animals can derive from tech. His quarters at the zoo, where he lives alone, offer few of the thrills he’d experience in the wild—chasing after live rodents and birds, wooing mates, fleeing wolves and bears. In the past, when I’d come to his enclosure as a visitor to the zoo, he had always been snoozing; simpler toys rarely captured his attention for more than a few minutes at a time, his keepers told me. This time, he trotted. He sniffed. He wriggled his tail. He reared up on his hind legs and used his long-clawed front paws to spin the pink wheel of his puzzle counterclockwise, then clockwise, until it yielded crunchy bits of kibble. When Kleinberger’s team first approached Pete Costello, the assistant curator at Stone Zoo, and his colleagues about the prospect of introducing the zoo’s residents to technology, his mind swam with images of creatures clutching iPads, doomscrolling their days away, he told me. But as Hudson spun his box—which can be toggled up and down through four levels of difficulty and programmed to release food at set intervals—I glanced over at Costello and the other keepers. They were beaming like proud parents and complimenting the fox’s deft moves: Ooh, good spin … Yup, he’s got it right away! Regardless of how well wild animals are cared for in captivity, their lives by definition shrink. In enclosures, removed from nature’s shifting circumstances and perils, and, often, the company of their own kind, they “lead very impoverished lives,” Clara Mancini, a pioneering researcher in the field of animal-computer interaction at the Open University, in the United Kingdom, told me. Technology may at first seem to make captive animals’ lives more artificial. But it’s starting to look like a good way to coax them into behaving more wildly. The field of technological enrichment for animals might have taken off decades earlier, Joey Golden, the curator of animal-behavior programs at the Maryland Zoo, told me. In the 1970s and ’80s, for instance, the scientist Hal Markowitz tinkered with devices that taught Diana monkeys to dispense food from a vending machine, or flung “flying meatballs” for servals. The animals were, by all appearances, interested in Markowitz’s devices. But “what he was building,” Golden said, “was incredibly artificial” to others in the research community who were focused on better replicating wild habitats. Animal-behavior researchers have since come around to the idea that the aesthetics of an animal’s surroundings are less important than how well those surroundings serve them. Creatures need the unpredictability of the natural world; in the monotony of captivity, where humans are in charge, a device that can shift with an animal’s needs can offer that creature “a little more control,” Kleinberger told me. A wobbly chew toy stuffed with a mini meatball might entertain a fox like Hudson for a few minutes. But it can’t operate on a shifting schedule or become more difficult as the user learns its quirks—unlike a teched-up puzzle feeder. Kleinberger got her start by creating voice-technology tools for musicians and people who stutter. But several years ago, keepers at the San Diego Zoo asked if she would develop a device to help a beats-loving hyacinth macaw named Sampson control his own playlist. Kleinberger was pleased to see not only that the device not only gave Sampson access to the tunes he wanted to hear, but that he’d bob his head along with the music to attract the types of zoo visitors whom he seemed to particularly enjoy. “He really liked children or people wearing red T-shirts,” Kleinberger said. Sampson, in effect, had made the technology his own. Determining what animals may get out of different technologies requires some guesses and leaps. At Stone Zoo, a pair of black bears, Smoky and Bubba, are certainly interested in the device that Kleinberger’s team built for them—a motion-sensitive machine that blows scented bubbles. The day I visited, both bears lumbered over to the lemon-scented, then bacon-scented bubbles streaming into their enclosure; they took big, huffing whiffs; flicked their ears; and occasionally licked their chops. The bubbles were stimulating the bears’ keen noses, engaging a sense they might not otherwise use very often. But, of course, the bears couldn’t tell us how much they actually liked the experience. When I asked Mancini how researchers know the full effects that technology has on other creatures, her answer was simple: “We don’t.” Animal behaviorists generally don’t like to use words such as enjoyment and boredom to describe their study subjects. “Those are very human concepts,” Kleinberger said. But if the goal is to improve captive animals’ lives, sentiments at least akin to enjoyment and boredom are what enrichment researchers must try to measure. Scientists are left to use the animals’ actions as a code, Malu Celli, Zoo New England’s vice president of animal care, told me. Researchers look for certain behaviors that they might feel are lacking in captivity: more swimming by a penguin, more walking by a bear, more foraging by a fox. They’ll also pay attention to how a creature’s senses are engaged—whether an animal’s ears lift or its eyes focus on a machine—while watching for subtle signs of distress. A positive result can be as simple as “Do they come back to it?” Kleinberger said. Or, presented with the same system, do they run away? Engagement doesn’t necessarily mean fulfillment. An animal that’s spending long periods of time with a screen might just be drawn in by the lights. And in the same way that chasing a laser might infuriate a cat who can never catch its blinking red “prey,” touch screens that ask animals to chase virtual objects might never satisfy predatory instincts. Tech might also suck up so much of an animal’s attention that it doesn’t do much of anything else, or engross a group of creatures so much that they begin to compete for access, Marcela Benítez, an animal-behavior researcher at Emory University, told me. At some zoos, in fact, caretakers have entirely banned smartphones around their great-ape enclosures out of concern that the animals might get too much screen time. Researchers are still trying to understand the extent to which different species can recognize two-dimensional images or videos of themselves or others of their own kind. Certain brainy creatures can be good candidates for screen-based interactions: Kleinberger and her colleagues have had success with a video-chatting system for pet parrots, highly social birds that commonly end up isolated in a human home. Many of the parrots they studied would chatter or sing loudly to each other through the screen. One parrot in the study, Kleinberger said, started flying more often after a series of calls with another bird that frequently flitted about. Some of the parrots, though, just lay down next to the device or fell asleep. Several of the scientists I spoke with for this story also happened to be parents of very young children; more than once, they compared the types of enrichment they were tinkering with to the games and toys they’d shared with their toddlers, for better or for worse. Some wondered about the potential for addiction, or even about skewing certain creatures’ perception of reality by overloading them with artifice. But projecting too much of humans’ own complicated relationships with technology onto animals can unnecessarily limit research. Just as animals might derive the same benefits that people do, they also might not experience the same harms. With Hudson, Kleinberger at first worried that the fox might become unhealthily obsessed with his puzzle box. But the presence of the device, she said, instead seemed to boost his enthusiasm for activity in general. It’s hard to say for sure, but maybe the technology reminded him that there was still fun to be had inside his artificial world.
The Atlantic
Voters Have a Message for Democrats
2026-09-08

Voters Have a Message for Democrats

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
During the early Trump era, focus groups taught me everything about the disconnect between Washington and what voters really care about. An early revelation came in July 2018; I had flown to Columbus, Ohio, to hear from a group of people who’d voted for Donald Trump in 2016, but only reluctantly. Three days before, Trump had participated in a diplomatic summit in Helsinki with Russian President Vladimir Putin. By that point, the U.S. intelligence community had publicly concluded that Putin had personally ordered a campaign to meddle in the 2016 presidential election for Trump’s direct benefit. At a press conference after their meeting, Trump was asked whether he believed his own intelligence agencies or the Russian president. “President Putin says it’s not Russia,” Trump responded. “I don’t see any reason why it would be.” And just like that, the president had undermined 80 years of American foreign policy, prompting a bipartisan meltdown in Washington. I was excited to hear what Trump-skeptical voters in the heartland thought about this epochal moment. These were the kinds of people who had been Reagan’s fiercest Cold Warriors, right? Surely they wouldn’t tolerate something like this from the American president. But when the moderator running the focus group asked the participants for their thoughts about Trump’s comments, he got a lot of blank stares. Even big news like this doesn’t always filter down to voters, so the moderator explained the incident. I figured that once the group understood what had happened, outrage would follow. Nope. One person said that Trump’s comments were “wrong,” but the feeling in the room was that of a collective shrug. The thing we were all freaking out about in D.C. had barely registered out there among the voters. Since that moment, I’ve conducted more than 400 focus groups with thousands of voters across the political spectrum. To my knowledge, this is the largest and most consistent qualitative study of voter sentiment during the Trump era. Here’s what I’ve learned: People are wild. They are full of contradictions. They lament our deep political divisions while raging about their political enemies. They believe in grace but cut their Trumpy brother out of their life forever. They’re married to an immigrant but voted for the guy who wants to deport millions of people who have the same immigration status as their wife. They hate the government and want to lower the national debt, but they also want the government to spend more to help them and their communities. They call themselves pro-life but believe in a woman’s right to choose. I could go on. Missy Ryan: What is the Democrats’ answer to ‘America First’? Listening to voters is a cheat code for understanding politics—democracy, after all, is the collective will of the voters—yet few people in politics do it. If you’re running a campaign, you might do a series of focus groups with voters in your district or state. If you’re a politician, you might go on “listening tours,” chat with people at the state fair, or host campaign events. But rarely do politicians and campaigns take the time to talk in-depth about why people feel the way they do. The reality is, the closer you get to institutional politics, the further you are from the people whom politics and government are supposed to serve. Washington is less a swamp than a bubble, inside of which the rest of America becomes an abstraction. I’m a former Republican who left the party after Trump took it over. I saw him for what he was: an existential threat to American liberal democracy. So I say this as someone who both crossed the political divide and still feels that Americans are underreacting to the acute threat of Trump and Trumpism: If Democrats want to win back power, they’re going to need to learn how to better relate to the American people, as people. For all of their nonlinear thinking and idiosyncratic views, voters have told me a couple of things over and over again: They think the system is broken, and they feel that they’ve been lied to in ways that make their life tangibly worse. Fewer of them now believe that if you work hard and play by the rules, you can get ahead in this country. Instead, they think that “establishment” politicians are benefiting from this system while everyone else loses out. Because of that, they want to place their trust in leaders who come across as “real people” who are genuinely interested in improving voters’ day-to-day life—not just winning and holding political office. In 2016, Republican voters looked at their party’s leadership—the Romneys, McCains, and Bushes—and decided they were done. Those leaders were too weak, too unwilling to fight, too committed to norms and processes that Democrats didn’t respect anyway. Republican voters hired a wrecking ball named Trump to knock it all down. Ten years later, I’m hearing similar sentiments from Democratic voters. They don’t trust Chuck Schumer, Hakeem Jeffries, or any of the old-guard party leaders who allowed Trump to take over America. They want their own wrecking ball. There’s a distinction here. Democrats don’t want Trump’s politics or even necessarily his all-out norm breaking. But they want his energy. They’re sick of a Democratic Party that does the functional equivalent of sending a strongly worded letter while democracy burns down around us. That tension—absolutely not, but also yes—is the defining mood among Democratic voters right now. They don’t want to become what they hate. But they are tired of losing to it. I don’t think that most Democratic elites understand this reality. In those circles, the dominant debate seems to be about whether the party should be more progressive or more moderate. The future of the party is seen as a policy question. But when I listen to Democratic voters, they’re not asking for ideological recalibration or even policy specifics. They trust that if the right person makes it into office, the policy questions will take care of themselves. Instead, the Democratic voters I’m listening to are almost universally asking a different question: Why aren’t you fighting harder? Last year, my team and I decided to run an experiment. We convened a couple of focus groups with Democrats who wanted the party to be more moderate, then a couple with Democrats who wanted the party to be more progressive. If I didn’t tell you which group was which, you would not be able to hear the difference by listening to them. Here’s a sampling from the more moderate group. Brandon from Georgia lamented that Democrats don’t “fight fire with fire,” saying, “Republicans will take an issue even if it’s completely fake news, and they’ll just beat it down the hatches.” Karen from Pennsylvania said that Jeffries is a “great guy” but that he is “too weak,” and “I think he’s just got to be more forceful, and maybe even nasty.” Now here are some of the Democrats who want the party to be more progressive. Jeffrey from Georgia: “I mean, the party just seems to me like it’s like a turtle that’s been turned on its back, you know. It’s just kind of flailing around hoping that it could, you know, somehow right itself.” Mary from Michigan said: “Them actually showing a spine would be a delightful change of pace. I have been calling my congresspeople every damn day telling them to not go along with the Cabinet approvals, to not rubber-stamp things just because you’re in the minority. They have not done a single thing.” These voters didn’t speak in terms of wanting the party to be more to the left or more to the center. They all just wanted Democrats to be more aggressive. Democratic voters have watched their party lose battles that they thought were already settled—abortion rights, voting rights, basic democratic norms—and they’re done with the old approach. They’ve watched Democratic politicians continue to engage with MAGA Republicans like it’s 2012 and Mitt Romney is running the GOP. Democratic voters recognize that the old rules don’t apply anymore. It’s not clear that their leaders have caught up to that reality. How can Democrats make a change? One of the things that Trump did—and I mean this not as a value judgment but as a factual matter—was transform the relationship between the president and the public. He made it direct, constant, intimate, and unavoidable. Through his tweets and his rallies and his Fox News call-ins and his Truth Social posts, Trump is in people’s face all the time. You know what he thinks about everything. You know when he sleeps by the time stamps on his posts. You know whom he’s mad at today. As a result, voters feel like they know him—like they’re in on the joke, part of the movement, personally connected to power. Many Democratic Party figures hate this. It feels to them like a debasement of the office, and, in my view, they’re right. Listen: A battle for the future of the Democratic party Although Trump’s constant communication was initially a shock to the body politic, voters developed a tolerance for it—and they have come to expect it. Whoever runs on the Democratic side in 2028 will have to be culturally omnipresent too. They shouldn’t mimic Trump’s chaos and cruelty. But they’ll have to play along with the electorate’s desire to know where they stand on everything, all the time. They’ll need a direct relationship with the voters that bypasses what’s left of media gatekeepers. Julie, a Democrat from Arizona, said this about her party’s leaders in an April 2025 focus group: “They didn’t make people connect to them emotionally enough. And we need that emotional connection. If you look at the MAGA party, they have, albeit a bit psychotically, a very emotional connection. And we need that.” She’s recognizing that Trump built something that Democrats haven’t—a bond that goes beyond policy positions. Looking ahead to the midterms and the 2028 presidential election, I cannot emphasize this enough: Donald Trump is not 10 feet tall, and MAGA is not invincible. Remember Arnold Schwarzenegger in Predator? “If it bleeds, we can kill it,” he said. MAGA might have all of the power right now, but it’s bleeding. Voters in the focus groups have talked about four main areas where the GOP is weakest, giving Democrats an opening. The first is immigration. This was supposed to be Trump’s strength, and in many ways, it still is; voters consistently rate Republicans more highly on border security. But there’s a gap between what voters wanted and what Trump has delivered. Lauren, a Trump voter from Florida, captured this: “I feel like we have so many hardworking immigrants,” she said. She wants the government to “leave those people alone. Please target people entering the country now, and people—criminals—who are here now illegally. I feel like that should be our first point of action, not going into elementary schools and picking kids out of seats.” Many of the voters who say this kind of thing aren’t open-borders liberals. They are people who wanted border security, but they believe that Trump has gone too far—that there’s a difference between strength and cruelty. Trump is also vulnerable on the issue of corruption. Voters who expected him to fight for them are discovering that he’s been cleaning up for himself. Olan from Georgia, a 2020 Joe Biden voter and 2024 Trump voter, noted the skeeviness of Trump accepting a jet from the government of Qatar: “Everything he’s doing is basically enriching his family. I’m not saying, ‘Hey, you’re not supposed to make money,’ but I thought we’re supposed to be helping the middle class here.” Jeffrey Epstein also remains a weak spot for Trump. “Why did he campaign on it that he’s gonna get in there and he’s gonna release the Epstein files?” Melissa, a two-time Trump voter from Pennsylvania, wondered in November. “Usually he’s super transparent, and what he says he’s gonna do, he’s gonna do.” Sam, a Gen Z Trump voter from Minnesota, said early this year, “By dragging his feet on it, it’s really like, man, some people might really just be above the law, and it’s not something that sits well with me.” Finally, there’s the economy. What I hear from 2024 Trump voters in focus groups is simple: Trump isn’t doing the things that he told Americans he would do to fix prices. In particular, young people who voted for Trump have said that they believed him during the campaign when he promised to “build the greatest economy in the history of the world.” When he didn’t, they felt duped and let down. “There are things that are very disappointing and very rough right now,” Kim from Virginia said. “Overall, I think the job market is really hard right now,” Allison from New York said. I know this might seem obvious to say, but the economy and cost of living remain as important as ever to voters, even while many of the long-standing rules of politics and voter behavior are shifting. Good news for the Democrats: This is the kind of moment in which new leaders emerge. The old guard is exhausted. The appetite for change is overwhelming. People are ready—desperate, even—for something new. When I ask voters which potential candidates they’re excited about, they don’t cite boring, by-the-book, geriatric politicians. They talk about fighters who go viral. Mike, a Massachusetts Democrat, said, “Jon Ossoff is blowing me away. He doesn’t go on and on. He’s not apologetic. He’s not defensive. He simplifies things.” Mike likes that Ossoff “just brings it down to, like, a bullet point.” Idress Kahloon: The Democrats can’t just laugh off ‘Woke 1’ Bryan, a New York Democrat, said, “Someone like Pete Buttigieg is a fantastic communicator, articulates well, and is willing to go on as many right-wing areas and interview formats, just because he can, because he knows how to articulate and he knows how to speak to both sides.” Leonard, another New York Democrat, praised New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani. “Sometimes you absolutely need a new voice, someone who actually can come in there as a renegade and not be beholden to the past, and come in there as an innovator and aim to get some things done.” Ultimately, he said, “sometimes you just need that kind of a shake-up.” And Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez’s name gets brought up all the time. Adrien, a Pennsylvania Democrat, said, “She’s charismatic. She actually gives a crap about people. She knows what it’s like to be working class.” Petra, another Democrat, agreed: “She has a backbone. She’s a strong, strong woman. She’s not afraid to tell it like it is.” Notice that this kind of appeal isn’t limited to either the party’s left wing or its center. It’s more about coming across as an authentic messenger who cares about the issues that ordinary people also care about—not the interminable moderate-versus-progressive policy discourse that animates professional political obsessives. I know from listening to voters that they recognize a fighter when they see one. They respond to authenticity, to energy, to the sense that a leader gives a damn. And they can tell when someone is genuinely committed to changing things. The Democratic candidates who succeed in the coming years will be the ones who articulate clearly why Trump and his movement have failed voters. They will seize on the contradictions and broken promises of the “America First” movement and build a platform that feels responsive to people’s everyday needs. They’ll center economic concerns, opportunity, the idea of rebuilding the American dream as part of a concrete plan for how people’s lives can get better. And they’ll do it with urgency—not because they want to be like Trump but because they understand that these times demand wartime generals, not peacetime diplomats: leaders who recognize that American democracy is in a fight for its survival and are willing to act like it. The voters are ready. The question is whether the Democratic Party will listen.
