Misunderstanding Norman Rockwell
2026-09-08

Misunderstanding Norman Rockwell

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
In 1976, as the country commemorated its bicentennial, an aging Norman Rockwell, then at the tail end of his career as America’s preeminent illustrator, painted his last magazine cover, Liberty Bell (Celebration). It was a tender—and notably low-key—tribute to national unity that doubled as a capstone for his body of work. For a decades-long stretch in the mid-20th century, the illustrator’s memorable images of smiling families and gentle, small-town interactions had filled the pages of national magazines, serving up a portrait of American life that was upbeat, idealistic, and reassuring. It’s a vision that has felt far away amid this year’s celebrations, once again, of the nation’s birthday. But the Rockwellian vision of America has felt distant for a long while. That distance is why we have songs such as Lana Del Rey’s “Norman Fucking Rockwell.” It’s why the phrase like a Rockwell painting is often deployed, not simply as a descriptive but as an implicit critique of naive civic optimism. And it’s why Rockwell has served as enduring grist for artists who, although they have been influenced by his visual iconography, have at the same time been compelled to give voice to some of the more disturbing impulses that have shaped the American psyche. No one did this more compellingly than David Lynch, the director who, over and over, turned Rockwell’s depiction of American innocence on its head. Lynch liked to recount in interviews a comment that a sound mixer on one of his films had once made to him: that his work was “like Norman Rockwell meets Hieronymus Bosch.” The filmmaker wanted to take Rockwell’s attractive but brittle surfaces, break them open, and plunge us into something deeper and darker. Understanding Lynch, in other words, is a way of understanding Rockwell. In particular, it’s a way of understanding one of the tragic effects of Rockwell’s influential vision: It’s so relentlessly benign and so morally pristine that it’s impossible to live up to. It’s an albatross slung over America’s shoulders. Early in Lynch’s masterpiece Mulholland Drive, which turns 25 this year, is a scene that doubles as one of Lynch’s many loving homages to Rockwell. The bright-faced, wide-eyed Betty (played by Naomi Watts) has arrived in Los Angeles, where she wants to become a movie star. On the flight over, she befriended an older couple, who walk with her through the airport. “Well, it’s time to say goodbye, Betty,” the old lady says. “It’s been so nice traveling with you.” “Thank you, Irene. I’m so excited and nervous. It was so good to have you to talk to.” “Remember, I’ll be watching for you on the big screen.” They part, whereupon a look of horror cavorts across Betty’s face: Her bags are no longer at her feet! “My bags!” she says aloud. But her panic lasts only a moment. The Rockwellian universe is briskly restored: A friendly cab driver is putting her luggage in the trunk of his taxi. “Where to?” he asks. Only now, having toyed with us, does Lynch signal that everything we have just witnessed—the “kindness, comedy, and forgiving tristesse” that the critic Dave Hickey rightly saw as a hallmark of Rockwell’s sensibility—has been subject to a menacing undertow. Lynch returns us to the old couple, now leaving the airport inside a black limo. Strapped snugly into their seats, they’re reminiscent of overgrown children; their old heads swivel back and forth in slow motion like laughing clowns’, and their twinkling smiles suggest something just short of psychopathy. Their grins are so unkillable, in fact, that what they most powerfully recall to me are the faces that crowd around the Thanksgiving table in one of Rockwell’s most famous images: Freedom From Want. This is one of four paintings he made from 1942 to 1943 to illustrate the “Four Freedoms” laid out by Franklin D. Roosevelt on January 6, 1941, when he provided the moral framework for America’s involvement in World War II. (The other three are Freedom of Speech, Freedom of Worship, and Freedom From Fear.) If you look closely at Freedom From Want, which is on permanent display at the Norman Rockwell Museum, in Stockbridge, Massachusetts, you see first how utterly brilliant Rockwell was. Perhaps most striking is how monochromatic large parts of the painting are. There’s the opaque white tablecloth, a white apron, and translucent white curtains—but within all of that white is an astonishing level of variety. The round plates are glossy. The creased tablecloth is matte. A silver tureen is so shiny that it gleams white. The transparent water glasses cast silvery reflections with white highlights. And those curtains are gauzily radiant. It’s a bravura display. Is it also somewhat stiff and sepulchral? It is! And this could kill the whole painting, except that against this almost fanatically pure still life, Rockwell—following the same principle of contrasts at the heart of Lynch’s aesthetic—sets the sparkling, animated choreography of the family. Cut off from their bodies by the picture’s vertical edges, their love-flushed heads lean in, drawn together by high spirits, humor, and the same intergenerational respect Lynch wanted us to feel between Betty and Irene in Mulholland Drive. Rockwell studied faces closely, including his own in the mirror. (That’s him smiling out of the painting’s lower right corner, like Lynch popping up as a hard-of-hearing FBI chief on Twin Peaks.) Good illustrators use facial expressions to convey action and consequence. Rockwell always sought maximum expression. Here, your gaze ping-pongs back and forth across the table, involuntarily weaving a lacy web of loving rapport. Still, the intensity of their expressions starts to feel uncanny. The teenage boy at the far end of the table has a smile so impeccably amiable that you idly wonder whether he might ever have lost his temper. At which point the pigtailed girl next to him, who stares straight at the viewer, starts to convey something latently demonic. Next to her, you notice with a start, is a wrinkled and waxy, almost clawlike hand, which presumably belongs to the venerable paterfamilias who stands at the head of the table, but which is cut off from his body by the platter carrying the turkey. If it’s easy to imagine the whole scene as a movie, slowed down, subtly sinister—like the scene with the grinning retirees in Mulholland Drive—that’s because Lynch’s work so effectively gives the lie to Rockwell’s. Read: David Lynch was America’s cinematic poet In the good-humored and reassuringly rational world that Rockwell constructed, there was no such thing as evil. His portraits of everyday life included pratfalls, minor moral lapses, and some genuine pain, but the arc of history was always bending the right way. In its fundamental integrity, Rockwellian America was as immaculate and charismatic as that Thanksgiving table. What is clear today is that the strain, so many decades on, of trying to make America conform to Rockwell’s softly arcadian image is too great. It’s a burden the nation cannot collectively handle and probably never could—which is why it continually demands forms of cultural release. These, over the years, have come in the form of not just Lynch’s creations but also novels such as Portnoy’s Complaint and American Psycho, and films such as Apocalypse Now. All are explosions of irrationality designed to release us from the whole Rockwellian racket and to renew our connection to darker truths. It’s not that Lynch’s take on America was any more real than Rockwell’s. It’s that his films expressed the absurdity of trying to make actual life match the illustrator’s gorgeous yet finally intolerable image of pleasantness and perfectibility. Since Rockwell’s heyday, this image has loomed over America like an impossibly kind, wise, and accomplished parent—and like a silent rebuke. On your way to the Norman Rockwell Museum, you may pass through the nearby town of Stockbridge, where Rockwell lived for a quarter century. He lovingly rendered its Main Street in a Christmas Eve scene painted in 1967, a year that also saw the Summer of Love, the release of Sgt. Pepper’s Lonely Hearts Club Band, and another 100,000 American troops sent to Vietnam. Fifty years ago, during the nation’s bicentennial celebrations (and two years before Rockwell died), Stockbridge staged a parade of floats with tableaux inspired by the illustrator. The procession, in which 2,000 people participated, was attended by more than 10,000. No other American artist has ever received such a tribute. The most extraordinary room at the Rockwell museum is always the basement gallery, which is festooned with the artist’s Saturday Evening Post covers—more than 300 of them. Rockwell first walked into the offices of the mass-circulation weekly in 1916, carrying a portfolio of his artwork. His timing was excellent: This was near the beginning of a great era in illustration, when advances in printing technology transformed large-scale color illustration into an essential aspect of communication. His talent was quickly rewarded. Within four years, Rockwell had become the Post’s leading illustrator. By 1925, he was known around the nation, and before the Depression was over, he had been all but canonized—America’s leading “Painter-Patriot,” as his biographer Deborah Solomon wrote, its paragon of picture-making. Rockwell achieved his status by painting small-town America. “I do ordinary people in everyday situations, and that’s about all I do,” he said in his 1960 memoir, My Adventures as an Illustrator. He was so successful, and so much better known than just about any other American artist, that people had great sport comparing him with his avant-garde peers in the art world. (This reputation gave rise to some amusing images: Rockwell’s The Connoisseur, from 1962, for instance, shows a portly, fastidious-looking man in a light-gray suit standing in front of a parti-color splatter painting in the manner of Jackson Pollock. When he saw The Connoisseur, Willem de Kooning, Pollock’s friend and rival, quipped that the illustrator had done it better than Pollock.) But Rockwell never pretended to be other than he was: an artist paid to make images in service to stories. Content to be called an illustrator, he was nevertheless unsure about the difference between what he did and fine art. His confusion was legitimate. Illustration, after all, is not inherently better or worse than any other style of art. Rembrandt was, to a large extent, an illustrator, a visual artist involved in the challenge of dramatizing Biblical stories; so was Winslow Homer, and not just in his magazine work. Narrative art is difficult for the obvious reason that it’s hard to show cause and effect (the core engine of narrative) with a single image frozen in time. The illustrator’s art lies in implying both cause and effect, so that a story—with its conventional, if artificial, movement from obscured buildup to crystalline consequence—unfolds in the viewer’s mind even as the image remains static. At this, Rockwell was brilliant. Still, professional illustrators are usually beholden to the directives of those who publish their work. In Rockwell’s case, this meant 47 years following the dictates of the Post, a magazine that sold 2 million copies a week and was avidly read by many more people. For decades, it also had an abhorrent policy (against which Rockwell chafed more and more until he finally severed ties with the magazine) of not depicting Black people except in subservient roles. After he left, Rockwell felt freer to address civil-rights-era issues, especially desegregation, which he did in such celebrated paintings as New Kids in the Neighborhood and The Problem We All Live With, his depiction, for Look magazine, of Ruby Bridges being escorted by federal marshals to her previously all-white school. To see the Post covers gathered together is to discern a cumulative, if highly prejudicial, portrait of America spanning more than four decades. Was it a portrait that helped America understand itself? Despite its cheesiness, and notwithstanding the Post’s appalling racism, I think it did—not just because Rockwell was so good at what he did but also because he was an artist in whom humor, grace, and loving appreciation easily commingled. These attributes were tethered, moreover, to a larger civic-mindedness. His best paintings are not only ingeniously constructed and brilliantly colored—they’re also richly humane. Stories are connected to our curiosity about how the world works, and for Rockwell, this curiosity was rooted in everyday life. “The commonplaces of America are to me the richest subjects in art,” he wrote in 1936. “Boys batting flies on vacant lots; little girls playing jacks on the front steps; old men plodding home at twilight, umbrellas in hand—all of these things arouse feeling in me. Commonplaces never become tiresome. It is we who become tired when we cease to become curious and appreciative.” Rockwell’s portrait of life in America is certainly kitsch. (Kitsch, Milan Kundera wrote, is “the absolute denial of shit, in both the literal and figurative sense of the word”; it “excludes everything from its purview which is essentially unacceptable in human existence.”) Still, his vision has had an enduring afterlife. Inevitably, its narrative potency—in particular its appeal to nostalgia—has been exploited across the political sphere. A Forbes columnist writing in 2012, more than three decades after the illustrator’s death, described the GOP primaries that year as “a process by which the candidate who demonstrates the most willingness to bring back Norman Rockwell’s America wins.” In 2020, shortly after he was elected, President Biden posted on Instagram a Hollywood ad agency’s reimagining of Rockwell’s United Nations mosaic, The Golden Rule, populated (in both the original and the adaptation) by a diverse group of Americans. And last year, President Trump’s Department of Homeland Security posted—without permission, prompting uproar—several of Rockwell’s paintings (including Salute the Flag and Working On the Statue of Liberty) to its social-media account. But it is not just this country’s politicians who are in Rockwell’s thrall. The painter’s affable pictorial rhetoric has cast a spell over holiday celebrations, modes of patriotic display, civic relations, even family relations. He was such a powerful image-maker that he created prototypes—ideals, really—of how life ought to be. And his work leaves a lingering sense that, if you want to be a happy American, you must (consciously or otherwise) reenact Rockwellian rituals. What Rockwell visualized was, of course, suffocatingly narrow. The critic Robert Hughes once called it “a dream world of small-town America.” In this America, Hughes wrote, “kids did not snort angel dust and get one another pregnant; they stole apples and swam in forbidden water holes but said grace before meals. All soldiers were nice boys from next door; all politicians were benevolent or harmlessly bumbling.” Big cities were subject to the roiling forces of unchecked capitalism and the blights of anonymity, rootlessness, and exploitation. Small towns, in Rockwell’s view, provided antidotes to all this. (He died well before the peak of deindustrialization and the first wave of the opioid epidemic.) They offered daily connection, neighborly interdependence, and, always, the lubricant of good humor (qualities that oiled Lynch’s twisted soap opera, Twin Peaks, too). Read: How Norman Rockwell captured the inner life of a nation To say that Rockwell’s America never existed is not to say that Rockwellian virtues do not exist; they do, all around us, every day. It’s only to acknowledge that the starched-white fiction these virtues cumulatively illustrate is by now so stained and sullied that just trying to keep up the pretense can trigger a kind of collective hysteria. The psychic pressure of trying to live up to an impossible ideal deeply interested Lynch. It produced his bifurcated visions of Rockwellian naivete and raw malevolence. In Rockwell, a diner is a place where a policeman can buy a runaway boy a milkshake before taking him home to his parents. In Lynch’s Mulholland Drive, Winkie’s Diner is where a tormenting nightmare becomes atrociously real. The night after I returned from Stockbridge, I rewatched Lynch’s Wild at Heart, his bizarre, hilarious, and very disturbing spin on Bonnie and Clyde. A lot of the action takes place in Big Tuna, a kind of anti-Stockbridge in the Texas badlands where evil thrives. The rusty, fish-shaped sign on the edge of town has been defaced with an expletive. The film’s main characters, Sailor and Lula (Nicholas Cage and Laura Dern), are deliberately cartoonlike, avatars of innocence transplanted to a context so malignant that it eats away at their mutual infatuation. When Lula picks up Sailor from prison, she brings him his favorite snakeskin jacket. “Did I ever tell you that this here jacket represents a symbol of my individuality and my belief in personal freedom?” he declares, like a third-grader reciting the Pledge of Allegiance. “About 50,000 times!” answers Lula, with no hint of a rebuke, only adoration. But that Rockwellian earnestness is no match for a setting corrupted by a hit man, a depraved mother, and the sniggering malevolence of Willem Dafoe. When Sailor confesses to having once worked for the man who murdered her father, Lula becomes pensive: “It’s just shocking,” she says, “when things aren’t the way you thought they were.” Rockwell expressed a similar sentiment before he died. It had been his idea to paint the Four Freedoms. He first took the proposal to the United States Office of War Information, where it was rejected, but after the paintings were published in the Post and received a huge response (60,000 letters were sent in by readers), the OWI reversed course and printed 2.5 million posters of the paintings, which the agency used to sell war bonds. In 1977, near the end of his life, Rockwell said: “I really believed that the war against Hitler would bring the Four Freedoms to everyone. But I couldn’t paint that today. I just don’t believe it. I was doing this best-possible-world, Santa-down-the-chimney, lovely-kids-adoring-their-kindly-grandpa sort of thing. And I liked it, but now I’m sick of it.” Lynch saw and loved Rockwell’s America—really loved it, as a child loves their parents—but something compelled him to break its surface and turn it violently inside out. This was a psychic and an artistic reaction, which is very different from a civic or political one. Maybe what America has to do with Norman Rockwell, as all children must eventually do with their parents, is figure out how to love him and leave him at the same time.

A War of Assassinations
2026-09-08

A War of Assassinations

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for our newsletter about national security here. The latest Iranian assassination threat arrived last month as a video called “Where to Kill Barron Trump,” promising $10 million for the murder of the U.S. president’s youngest child. Less than 24 hours later, on social media, the State Department re-upped its own $10 million bounty for “information” about five leaders of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, including Majid Khademi, the intelligence-office commander. The State Department offer, part of its Rewards for Justice program, first circulated on March 20. A few weeks later, the Israeli government took credit for killing Khademi in an air strike on Tehran, adding an ominous note to the U.S. government’s decision to recirculate the poster. I asked the State Department whether this was deliberate, so soon after Iran’s announcement of a $10 million bounty for the president’s son. An official declined to comment on “internal deliberations” but noted that information obtained through the Rewards for Justice program can be used to “support a range of U.S. government responses to disrupt threats, consistent with U.S. law and policy.” Threats of assassination have, from the start, been dark and disruptive subplots of the U.S.-Iran war. Both sides have made the tactic central to their national strategies, though the executions have been rather one-sided, as President Trump and his Israeli allies have overseen the widespread killing of Iran’s senior leadership. The impact on the American government has nonetheless been profound. “We are aware of ongoing threats from the Iranian regime and its proxies and take them extremely seriously,” the State Department spokesperson Tommy Pigott told me in a written statement. “We will not hesitate to act to protect U.S. government officials, current and former, and their families.” Iran clearly sees the propaganda value of threatening American officials. Murals in Tehran now depict Trump, members of his family, and other senior U.S. officials in coffins. Hey Terrorist! Get Ready To Die, reads one apparently AI-generated billboard that shows Trump looking skyward on a golf course. Iran’s army chief promised a $30,000 bounty last month to any Iranian man who kills or captures a U.S. service member, without specifying a target location. (Women would be paid twice as much.) In addition to threatening Barron, Iranian outlets have released a “How to Kill Melania” video about the first lady, and published a target list of multiple world leaders and U.S. Cabinet members. “The criminal, disgraceful murderers of the martyred Leader, whose names are fully documented from the highest to the lowest ranks, will carry their dream of a peaceful death in bed to the grave,” Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei, the nation’s supreme leader, said in a written statement on July 11. He succeeded his father, Ayatollah Ali Khamenei, who was assassinated by Israeli air strikes on his compound on the first day of the war. But the Iranian campaign is not limited to posturing. Last month, Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced that “Iran tried to murder” one of his sons, an apparent reference to 35-year-old Yair Netanyahu. In July, Israeli intelligence described an Iranian plot to use a French resident, Servet Ozkur, to recruit assassins to target senior officials in Israel. Iran has repeatedly sent assassins to kill Trump and other U.S. officials, according to the Justice Department, as punishment for Trump’s 2020 order to assassinate the Iranian military commander Qassem Soleimani. About a week after the start of the Iran war this year, a federal jury in Brooklyn convicted Asif Merchant, the architect of one of two alleged Iranian plots in the United States. Prosecutors claimed that Merchant, a Pakistani national trained by Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, had entered the United States with orders to recruit “mafia” hit men. But Merchant ended up speaking with undercover officers, telling them they would receive instructions on the target after he left the country. He was arrested before he made it to the airport and faces a sentence of up to life in prison. Assassination was clearly on Trump’s mind when the elder Khamenei was killed. “I got him before he got me,” Trump told ABC News’s Jonathan Karl in March. “They tried twice. Well, I got him first.” The U.S. presidency has long been a dangerous job. Trump has faced at least three assassination attempts by domestic assailants. Those attacks, along with Iran’s threats, are reshaping the lives of the president and his advisers, and restricting Trump’s ability to interact with the country he governs. Earlier this year, at a summit in Ankara, Trump told reporters that his own chance of dying in office was 5.2 percent, dramatically higher than that of race-car drivers and bull riders. (He did not provide a source for his comparisons. Of the 45 men elected president, four, or about 8.9 percent, have been assassinated in office.) Trump said at the same appearance that “I’m No. 1 on the kill list for Iran.” Then the president turned his traveling press corps and two Cabinet officials into flying decoys when he snuck out a side door of Air Force One as it sat on the tarmac, to avoid a purported Iranian plot to target the plane. After a short ride in a catering truck, he boarded a different plane and flew to his next stop, in England. Read: The Qataris gave Trump an airplane. He crippled their country in return. The logistics of presidential travel are now far more burdensome than they were when Trump first took office. The Secret Service’s personal-protection budget has nearly doubled since 2016. New airspace restrictions have been imposed over Mar-a-Lago. Lafayette Park, just north of the White House, has been closed for months for security; there are plans to install new fencing that would further obstruct public access. The new White House ballroom, the U.S. Army has argued in court, is “essential to ensure uninterrupted command and control” given the new threats of a drone attack. A number of Cabinet officials and top White House aides now live on military bases to protect themselves and their families, in certain cases because of specific foreign threats. Other rank-and-file White House advisers, unnerved by a homegrown assassination attempt at the White House Correspondents’ Association Dinner in April, have receded from the city where they live. “People are just more careful about where they go and, frankly, how often they are out in public,” a White House official told me. All of this portends a grim future in which assassination moves from an extreme risk to a more commonplace threat. Perhaps that will lead to a reassessment of its use as both a tool of U.S. foreign policy and a way to menace an enemy’s senior officials. “At some stage, I do wonder whether politicians thinking about themselves actually come to the conclusion that they are signing their own death warrants or essentially undermining their own democracies” by promoting the extrajudicial killing of their enemies, Simon Ball, a professor at the University of Leeds, in England, and the author of Death to Order: A Modern History of Assassination, told me. He added that assassination threats are now such a regular fact of life that to have protection against them confers a macabre prestige. “Elite status is defined by whether you have protection from assassination,” Ball said. “If you do not have protection, you are not important. You are little people.” The assassination threats from Iran have had a big impact on Trump’s life, but administration officials contend that the threats have played no role in Trump’s decision to attack Iran, which he did in June 2025 and again in February, citing the need to prevent Iran from developing a nuclear weapon, among other reasons. “He has never equated their threats against him as a reason for doing this,” one senior administration official told me. “He views the threats against him as a part of the job.” But the military has made killing those who allegedly threaten the president part of the campaign. Four days after the conflict began, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth walked into the Pentagon with triumphant news about an Israeli strike the night before. The U.S. and Israel, he announced, had hunted down the “leader of the unit who attempted to assassinate President Trump.” On the one hand, Hegseth suggested that the strike was a personal victory for the president. “Iran tried to kill President Trump, and President Trump got the last laugh,” Hegseth told reporters. On the other hand, he said that Trump had not concerned himself with the strikes. Going after the president’s assassins, Hegseth said, “was not the focus of the effort by any stretch of the imagination, in fact, never raised by the president or anybody else.” Instead, by his account, it seems to have just sort of happened as a favor to the boss. “I ensured, and others ensured, that those who were responsible for that were eventually part of the target list,” Hegseth said. Trump has similarly blurred these lines, sometimes focusing on the threat and at other times dismissing it. After Cole Tomas Allen, a tutor from California, was apprehended while attempting to infiltrate the Correspondents’ Dinner armed with multiple guns and knives, Trump told reporters that “I don’t think about” the prospect of assassination, and “if I did, I wouldn’t be doing a very good job here.” But hours before B-2 bombers struck Iran in June last year, the president told reporters after disembarking Air Force One in New Jersey that they had put themselves in danger just by standing next to him. “Be careful,” he said. Trump has also made clear that any nation that assassinated him would face apocalyptic retaliation. “I’ve left instructions. If they do it, they get obliterated. There won’t be anything left,” he said of Iran two weeks after taking office last year. This July, hours before Mojtaba promised revenge for his father’s death, Trump posted on social media that even an attempted assassination on him would result in a military response: “Orders have already been given, and the U.S. Military is ready, willing, and able, for a one year period of time, subject to extension, to completely decimate and destroy all areas of Iran.” Iranian officials have been ambiguous about how seriously the U.S. should take its threats. Days after the Barron video began circulating on Tebyan.net, an arm of the Iranian government’s propaganda machine, the secretary of the Supreme National Security Council, Mohsen Rezaei, called the threats against Barron a lie and dismissed the alleged plot against Trump in Turkey as “false news.” But in the same interview with Lebanon’s Al-Manar TV, Rezaei—whose predecessor was killed by Israeli strikes in March—said Iran would send Netanyahu “to hell.” The claim received scant coverage. World leaders plotting to kill one another has become routine.

Powershell, surprisingly bad after about 50 or more lines. python and js is great. I think rust is fine, because the language has some hard rules, which are easy to follow and in case the AI does not follow them the compiler gives a hard error. AIs can iterate quite well on hard errors, the weakness is subtle mistakes that can't be caught by compilers, linters, etc. We have used LLM's to generate a lot of Rust very successfully. I would rather trust AI generating Rust than almost any other language because the Rust compiler is so fussy about types and object lifetimes that it gives the LLM far less opportunity to sneaky silly, hard to find, errors in. You will help establish how Rust is used in performance-sensitive and high-trust parts of the GPU stack, including the compiler and tooling support, system interfaces, engineering practices, and validation methods required for production deployment. Rust isn't trustworthy, it has no mechanism for independent validation or verification using cross compilation from multiple toolchains. Rust is often regarded as being “memory safe”, which it absolutely is not, since it has an “unsafe” keyword. You can't have an “unsafe” keyword and at the same time claim you're safe. Even without the unsafe keyword, you can still corrupt memory, which effectively makes all the trans-furry-handwaving ride or die, Rust religion devotion, pretty much on par with every other religion (See Lunduke for that reference). Why are they really using Rust? The spec for the language isn't finished, the tooling isn't finished, and it failed to meet the claims the evangelists scream about. At this point, you're picking Rust just for the handwaving and virtue signalling, it's not an engineering decision, no engineer worth the most basic qualification, would ever pick Rust, the joke has been over for a while. ” Rust is not incidental to this role. For what it's worth, London Black Cab Drivers (broadly regarded as something of a 'gold standard' for such things) will never take you across london by crossing the river twice. The river does bend around quite sharply, and for a few journeys it makes absolute sense, but they won't do it because people, being thicker than two short planks, complain that the driver is taking them the "long way", when actually they are keeping it as short as possible. These days with GPS maps and such like, I suspect thick people have offloaded even more of their thinking to their phone apps, so maybe nowadays things are different, I don't know. As for this story, I'd expect them to find me the quickest route, taking traffic into account. I don't know how these journeys are charged, but in the UK, it's per mile and per minute - that is, you get charged for sitting in traffic, as well as zipping down a trunk road. As such, I suspect most people would prefer the quickest route, given that it's highly unlikely to be materially much longer than the shortest route. This isn't a question of expand highway vs do nothing. It's a question of expanding highway vs doing something different that may have a better outcome (for both people who will use the system as well existing people on the highway who won't). Induced demand does not mean that building roads never reduces congestion; new capacity can provide substantial and sometimes lasting benefits. Rather, it means that traffic responds to changes in the cost and availability of road travel, just as traffic can decrease when road capacity is reduced through people changing routes, times, destinations, modes, or simply making fewer trips. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump Has Brutalized Blue States in His Second Term. There’s a Way for Democrats to Fight Back.
2026-09-08

Trump Has Brutalized Blue States in His Second Term. There’s a Way for Democrats to Fight Back.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
This is the second in a series of articles, “Try, Try Again?,” drawn from my new book—Great and Dangerous Offences: The Case For (and Against) Impeaching Trump a Third Time—due out in early 2027. The Constitution created a federal republic consisting of states with equal legal status. Under the 10th Amendment, Congress and the executive branch have only limited authority to intrude on the sovereign prerogatives of the states. Individual residents of those states are guaranteed equal protection of the laws. These are pillars of the American constitutional order. To subvert any of them is to subvert the Constitution itself, a subversion that, as the Framers insisted, is impeachable. If a president engages in a pattern of conduct that subordinates the rights and interests of individual states to his personal or partisan political interests, that would be impeachable. Likewise, if a president engages in a pattern of conduct that denies equal protection of the laws of the United States to the citizens of individual states in order to promote his personal or partisan political interests, that, too, would be impeachable. In his second term as president, Donald Trump has continuously done both. To understand how Trump has abused his power against the states, it’s important to understand the power the federal government—and the president, specifically—does have. For example, the widely varying geographic, demographic, social, and economic circumstances of the states has meant that they need different things from the central government. Thus, the unruly interaction of democratic politics with national government accepts a good deal of horse trading over which states will get various types of federal largesse and regulation, along with a degree of more or less constant disagreement between state and federal authorities over policy and resource allocation. In extreme cases, the federal government may employ coercion against states that are sufficiently out of harmony with national law and policy. If a state government openly violates federal law despite clear orders from the federal judiciary—as was the case of some Southern states during the civil rights conflicts of the 20th century—the national government may use appropriate measures, including force, to compel compliance with the law. This is not the current circumstance. Critically, the federal government has to enforce such measures itself rather than commandeering the states to do so. Congress lacks “the power to issue orders directly to the States.” It cannot compel states to issue regulations. It cannot command state officers to participate in or enforce federal laws under what is known as the anti-commandeering doctrine. While Congress can condition a state’s receipt of federal funds on compliance with specified conditions, there are limitations to this power. Congress’ conditions to states must be clear and unambiguous and must have some relationship to the purposes of the federal grants in question. The conditions cannot be imposed without a state’s knowing and voluntary consent. This means that Congress may not set conditions on dispersal of federal funds that effectively offer the state no realistic choice except compliance. While Congress has some authority to induce states to participate in federal initiatives, the president acting on his own authority has no such power. To be sure, the president and the executive branch are charged with lawfully implementing congressional directives about the dispersal of appropriated funds. But the president cannot discriminate between states in dispersing federal funds by imposing his own rules, policy stipulations, or personal preferences, particularly if they contradict or are unrelated to congressional funding directives. Moreover, the post–Civil War 14th Amendment made explicit that the individual inhabitants of states are entitled to equal treatment under national law, which means that people living in different states cannot be treated differently simply because one lives in a state politically aligned with the president and another does not. Trump has flagrantly violated these principles on multiple occasions during his second term. The first violation began on his first day back in office when he issued an executive order commanding that “sanctuary jurisdictions,” meaning states, counties, or municipalities that did not order their law-enforcement authorities to cooperate fully with Trump’s immigration-enforcement actions, “do not receive access to Federal funds.” The initial executive order was followed by two others with the same thrust. These orders are not based on any congressional enactment conditioning federal funding on state or local cooperation with federal immigration efforts. Trump renewed the threat to all federal funding in a statement in January 2026. Following Trump’s executive orders, various elements of the executive branch began to withhold funding to state, regional, and local governments deemed insufficiently compliant with, or openly resistant to, Trump’s immigration program. A number of cities and states that suffered funding cuts sued. At least one court has ruled the cuts illegal. Multiple cases are pending. It is relevant to any article of impeachment premised wholly or in part on coercion of the states over immigration policy that Trump tried essentially the same tactic in his first administration and was blocked by the federal courts. In short, the courts previously ruled that Trump impinged on the sovereignty of the states and ignored Congress’ power of the purse, but he came right back and did it again. This sort of repeat-offender behavior elevates what might otherwise be considered a simple policy or legal dispute into an impeachable offense. As troublesome as Trump’s extortion of state cooperation with his immigration efforts may be, it is at least connected to an articulable policy objective, however controversial. Two other types of denial of federal funds, grants, or programs are even more concerning, and even more compelling as grounds for impeachment. First, the Trump administration has actively discriminated against “blue” states by systemically withholding federal funding, grants, and programmatic assistance. The discrimination is self-evident from the available data. A review of presidential disaster declarations entitling states and localities to federal aid revealed that in Trump’s first 14 months in office, he approved a mere 23 percent of applications from states with a Democratic governor and two Democratic senators, while approving 89 percent of requests from states with a Republican governor and two Republican senators. Moreover, approvals of disaster declarations in Democratic states took twice as long as those to Republican states (80 days vs. 40 days). No pattern remotely similar has ever been observed for past presidents. Moreover, one need not rely on inferences from data. In the fall of 2025, Russell Vought, head of the Office of Management and Budget, announced cancellation of “nearly $8 billion” in grants for clean-energy programs authorized by Congress during the Biden administration—what Vought called the “Green New Scam.” The first indication that these cancellations, in addition to being open violations of Congress’ power of the purse, were based primarily on politics rather than climate policy was that he listed the states in which grants were canceled—and they included no states dominated by Republicans and instead included 16 of the 19 states that had voted for Kamala Harris in the 2024 election. Multiple states, a coalition including energy and environmental organizations and the city of St. Paul, Minnesota, a group of University of California researchers, and others sued the Trump administration over the cancellations. In pleadings filed in the researchers’ case, the U.S. Department of Energy formally stipulated that none of the 284 grants terminated in October 2025 were terminated “based on any programmatic, statutory, cost-reduction or performance-based factor.” The stipulation admitted that, “with one exception, the 284 terminated grants had a recipient location and/or at least one place of performance in a state that awarded its electoral votes to Kamala Harris in the 2024 election and has two Democratic-caucusing senators.” The stipulation concluded by confessing: DOE accepts that the inclusion of grants in the October notice tranche was based solely on the political identity of the grant recipient’s state. … DOE accepts that the differential treatment resulting in the October 2025 termination of Blue State grants and the non-termination of non-Blue State grants was not based on a rational connection between the recipient’s location and/or place of performance and DOE’s past or current agency priorities. As reported by the New York Times, at the same time as the administration was cancelling grants to “blue states,” it “left untouched hundreds of additional energy grants in states that were represented by Republicans and had backed Trump in the last election.” In short, the Trump administration has admitted using payment or withholding of congressionally appropriated funds to punish or retaliate against states and their inhabitants because, and only because, those states support the president’s political opposition. Second, Trump has gone beyond generic discrimination against states controlled by Democrats and, in at least one case, organized a multipronged attack on a single state because its criminal justice system prosecuted, convicted, and refused to release a Trump backer and election denier. Following the 2020 election, Tina Peters, the clerk and recorder of Mesa County, Colorado, was convicted of multiple felonies for breaching the county election systems in a supposed effort to prove that the election had been rigged and lying about it thereafter. In October 2024, Peters was sentenced to nine years in prison. In the years since 2020, Peters had become a prominent figure in the election-denialist circles supporting Trump, persisting in unsubstantiated claims that somehow Trump had been cheated out of the election against Joe Biden. When Peters was sentenced, Trump declared himself outraged. In August 2025, he demanded that Colorado release Peters, threatening that if it did not, “I am going to take harsh measures.” On Dec. 11, 2025, Trump issued what he characterized as a pardon of Peters. The president’s pardon power does not extend to state crimes. At the same time Trump was issuing his counterfeit pardon, he was reportedly fuming behind the scenes. On Dec. 15, 2025, White House special assistant Emily Underwood sent an email to five federal agencies organizing an urgent “brainstorm call” in order to, in her words, “prioritize Colorado for the purposes of ensuring grants and federal support are in line with administration priorities.” The next day, the Departments of Transportation and Energy, which had been invited to the “brainstorm,” canceled hundreds of millions of dollars in federal grants for projects in Colorado. In addition to the cuts in transportation and energy projects, two days after the “brainstorm” call, the Trump administration ordered that Boulder’s National Center for Atmospheric Research, a world leader in climate and earth systems research, be dismantled, shuttering some programs and moving others out of state. In late December, Trump denied two Colorado disaster relief requests stemming from wildfires and a flood. Also in December, the Department of Agriculture attacked Colorado’s federally supported Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program by imposing new requirements that would have been impossible to meet. On Dec. 30, 2025, Trump used his presidential veto to kill a congressionally authorized water project for the Eastern Plains region of Colorado. If there were any doubt about the connection between all these actions and Trump’s fury over the Peters prosecution, Trump removed them with a New Year’s Eve social media post in which he said, of Colorado Gov. Jared Polis and the Republican district attorney who prosecuted Peters: “I wish them only the worst,” and they should “rot in Hell.” Trump’s assault on Colorado was, if anything, more constitutionally egregious than his general attacks on other Democratic states. In his actions against other states, Trump and his minions were simply giving rein to general political animus or, in some cases, selectively applying his peculiar policy fixations, such as his hatred of clean energy. In Colorado, Trump employed the might of the federal government to coerce a state into granting undeserved personal relief to one properly convicted state felon. Worse, the felon in question was a figurehead in Trump’s ongoing effort to destroy confidence in the nation’s electoral system. Worst of all, the pressure worked: Polis granted Peters clemency in May and she was released in June, after having served less than a quarter of her original sentence. This was as blatant, and impeachable, an abuse of presidential power as can well be imagined. Multiple courts have entered orders blocking parts of Trump’s funding and program cancellations in states led by Democrats. In particular, in January 2026, a federal judge enjoined the assault on Colorado’s SNAP requirements. The move to dismantle NCAR was enjoined by the same federal court in June 2026; the court determined that the government’s action was procedurally “arbitrary and capricious” and that “retaliation played at least some role.” However, the question of whether presidential action is impeachable does not turn on the judgments of courts, particularly when the essential ground for an article of impeachment is the president’s active subversion of the basic constitutional design. It is absolutely plain that the president may not consciously discriminate against a state government or the residents of a state based purely on the partisan political alignment of the state’s residents or its government. Nor can a president enlist the power of the federal government to coerce a state’s justice system into granting special treatment to a personal favorite of the president, particularly if that favorite’s crime was committed in aid of the president’s own attack on the nation’s electoral system. Considered together, Trump’s partisan attacks on Democratic states and their residents amount to an abuse of presidential power sufficiently egregious and disruptive of constitutional order to merit impeachment.
